Yeah, uh… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly after late shifts. I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.

It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.

I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.

It’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.

So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.

I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”

Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.

If it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.

So I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly after late shifts. I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Not always right that second, because I’m kind of numb and tired, but after, when I’m sitting there with the wrappers or the containers, I feel ashamed. Like… disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I’m the guy people count on. I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do, I’ve always thought of myself as pretty disciplined. My dad was big on that too—if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, no excuses. So then sneaking food around late at night, eating in the car or out in the garage… it makes me feel like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating this extra food because you can't stop yourself, or is it more like you're making a choice to do it?\n\nPatient: It feels like I can’t stop, yeah. I mean, I know I’m the one buying it, so it sounds like a choice, but once I start… it’s like a switch flips.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, Roy, close the bag, go inside, just be done, and my hands are still reaching in. It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.

But then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.

So… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.

I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Not always right that second, because I’m kind of numb and tired, but after, when I’m sitting there with the wrappers or the containers, I feel ashamed. Like… disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I’m the guy people count on. I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do, I’ve always thought of myself as pretty disciplined. My dad was big on that too—if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, no excuses. So then sneaking food around late at night, eating in the car or out in the garage… it makes me feel like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating this extra food because you can't stop yourself, or is it more like you're making a choice to do it?\n\nPatient: It feels like I can’t stop, yeah. I mean, I know I’m the one buying it, so it sounds like a choice, but once I start… it’s like a switch flips.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, Roy, close the bag, go inside, just be done, and my hands are still reaching in. It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.

Like with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.

I’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.

And it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.

Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.

If I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.

It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Binge-Eating Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this extra food if you weren't working the late shifts\n\nPatient: I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.\n\nIf I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your eating habits would go back to normal if you were to switch to an earlier work shift?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Eating_Large_Amount\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that after late shifts I’ll stop for takeout and end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, and then still go home and clean out leftovers in the garage like it’s a second supper.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… but I don’t think it would just magically go back to normal. The late shift is kind of when it happens, you know? I get off and I’m tired, and I’ll stop for takeout thinking it’s just dinner, but then it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and end up eating leftovers out in the garage like it’s another whole meal.

I mean, if I got off earlier maybe I wouldn’t hit those same places, or I’d be home with my wife more at a normal dinner time. Back when I worked different hours, the routine was different—though that was years ago, and everything’s changed around the plant since then. But yeah, earlier shift might help. I just don’t know if it would fix it all by itself.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly after late shifts. I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Not always right that second, because I’m kind of numb and tired, but after, when I’m sitting there with the wrappers or the containers, I feel ashamed. Like… disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I’m the guy people count on. I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do, I’ve always thought of myself as pretty disciplined. My dad was big on that too—if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, no excuses. So then sneaking food around late at night, eating in the car or out in the garage… it makes me feel like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating this extra food because you can't stop yourself, or is it more like you're making a choice to do it?\n\nPatient: It feels like I can’t stop, yeah. I mean, I know I’m the one buying it, so it sounds like a choice, but once I start… it’s like a switch flips.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, Roy, close the bag, go inside, just be done, and my hands are still reaching in. It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this extra food if you weren't working the late shifts\n\nPatient: I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.\n\nIf I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would go back to normal if you were to switch to an earlier work shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe… but I don’t think it would just magically go back to normal. The late shift is kind of when it happens, you know? I get off and I’m tired, and I’ll stop for takeout thinking it’s just dinner, but then it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and end up eating leftovers out in the garage like it’s another whole meal.\n\nI mean, if I got off earlier maybe I wouldn’t hit those same places, or I’d be home with my wife more at a normal dinner time. Back when I worked different hours, the routine was different—though that was years ago, and everything’s changed around the plant since then. But yeah, earlier shift might help. I just don’t know if it would fix it all by itself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it like that.

I mean, everybody gets upset, right? Some days at the plant are just aggravating, machines acting up, people not doing what they’re supposed to—there was one guy on my line years ago who could make a simple job take twice as long, and it would drive me nuts. But whether that makes me eat more… I’m not sure.

It feels more tied to being tired and getting off late, at least that’s what I notice. I’m not saying emotions have nothing to do with it, I just don’t know that I can point to feeling down and say, yeah, that’s when it happens.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly after late shifts. I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Not always right that second, because I’m kind of numb and tired, but after, when I’m sitting there with the wrappers or the containers, I feel ashamed. Like… disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I’m the guy people count on. I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do, I’ve always thought of myself as pretty disciplined. My dad was big on that too—if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, no excuses. So then sneaking food around late at night, eating in the car or out in the garage… it makes me feel like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating this extra food because you can't stop yourself, or is it more like you're making a choice to do it?\n\nPatient: It feels like I can’t stop, yeah. I mean, I know I’m the one buying it, so it sounds like a choice, but once I start… it’s like a switch flips.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, Roy, close the bag, go inside, just be done, and my hands are still reaching in. It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this extra food if you weren't working the late shifts\n\nPatient: I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.\n\nIf I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would go back to normal if you were to switch to an earlier work shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe… but I don’t think it would just magically go back to normal. The late shift is kind of when it happens, you know? I get off and I’m tired, and I’ll stop for takeout thinking it’s just dinner, but then it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and end up eating leftovers out in the garage like it’s another whole meal.\n\nI mean, if I got off earlier maybe I wouldn’t hit those same places, or I’d be home with my wife more at a normal dinner time. Back when I worked different hours, the routine was different—though that was years ago, and everything’s changed around the plant since then. But yeah, earlier shift might help. I just don’t know if it would fix it all by itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling upset or emotionally down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nI mean, everybody gets upset, right? Some days at the plant are just aggravating, machines acting up, people not doing what they’re supposed to—there was one guy on my line years ago who could make a simple job take twice as long, and it would drive me nuts. But whether that makes me eat more… I’m not sure.\n\nIt feels more tied to being tired and getting off late, at least that’s what I notice. I’m not saying emotions have nothing to do with it, I just don’t know that I can point to feeling down and say, yeah, that’s when it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure. I mean, daytime shifts might help some, just because I wouldn’t be getting off so late and driving past all those places when I’m already wiped out.

I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this extra food if you weren't working the late shifts\n\nPatient: I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.\n\nIf I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would go back to normal if you were to switch to an earlier work shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe… but I don’t think it would just magically go back to normal. The late shift is kind of when it happens, you know? I get off and I’m tired, and I’ll stop for takeout thinking it’s just dinner, but then it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and end up eating leftovers out in the garage like it’s another whole meal.\n\nI mean, if I got off earlier maybe I wouldn’t hit those same places, or I’d be home with my wife more at a normal dinner time. Back when I worked different hours, the routine was different—though that was years ago, and everything’s changed around the plant since then. But yeah, earlier shift might help. I just don’t know if it would fix it all by itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling upset or emotionally down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nI mean, everybody gets upset, right? Some days at the plant are just aggravating, machines acting up, people not doing what they’re supposed to—there was one guy on my line years ago who could make a simple job take twice as long, and it would drive me nuts. But whether that makes me eat more… I’m not sure.\n\nIt feels more tied to being tired and getting off late, at least that’s what I notice. I’m not saying emotions have nothing to do with it, I just don’t know that I can point to feeling down and say, yeah, that’s when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if your work schedule changed to only daytime shifts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, daytime shifts might help some, just because I wouldn’t be getting off so late and driving past all those places when I’m already wiped out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means my eating would really change, or just change time of day, you know? Sometimes routines stick with you. Like when they switched our break times at the plant, everybody complained for weeks and then somehow people still found a way to do the same stuff, just at different times.\n\nSo… maybe it’d be better, but I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. Being tired feels like a big part of it, but I don’t know if taking that away would mean I wouldn’t do it.

Like, some nights I’m just worn out and I don’t feel like thinking, and that’s when it’s easy to pull into a drive-thru. But then there are times on days off where I’m not exactly exhausted, and I still end up wandering into the kitchen too much. So I don’t know how to separate it cleanly.

It’s kind of like at work—everybody blames the night shift for everything, but then you move something to days and some of the same problems follow you. So… maybe it would happen less if I wasn’t tired, but I can’t honestly say it wouldn’t happen.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly after late shifts. I’ll get off work and tell myself I’m just grabbing something quick, you know, like takeout on the way home, and then it turns into two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through leftovers in the garage like it’s a whole second supper.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so late shifts kind of mess with your head anyway, and there’s this burger place that stays open late near the plant—anyway, that’s… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Not always right that second, because I’m kind of numb and tired, but after, when I’m sitting there with the wrappers or the containers, I feel ashamed. Like… disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I’m the guy people count on. I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do, I’ve always thought of myself as pretty disciplined. My dad was big on that too—if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, no excuses. So then sneaking food around late at night, eating in the car or out in the garage… it makes me feel like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating this extra food because you can't stop yourself, or is it more like you're making a choice to do it?\n\nPatient: It feels like I can’t stop, yeah. I mean, I know I’m the one buying it, so it sounds like a choice, but once I start… it’s like a switch flips.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, Roy, close the bag, go inside, just be done, and my hands are still reaching in. It’s like when the line at work keeps moving and you just keep doing the next thing without really deciding each time—except this is food, and I hate that I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food when you're feeling stressed or anxious after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it stressed or anxious exactly. I mean, work can be a lot, sure, but it’s not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m just worn out, like my brain’s kind of off by the time I clock out. And then there’s the routine of driving past that place, the lights still on, smells coming through the window… Sorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed if it’s stress specifically. It’s more like late, tired, and I’m already halfway into it before I think too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating this extra food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the best way to put it. Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Roy, shut the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then somehow my hand’s still going back in for more. It’s embarrassing to say out loud. It reminds me a little of the line at work when the machine’s running and you just keep feeding parts through without thinking, except… I know that’s not really the same thing. With the food, I’m aware of it, but I’m not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating this extra food to try to feel better or improve your mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “This will cheer me up,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m tired, and it’s there, and then I’m doing it. Sometimes I guess it feels good for a minute, like when you finally sit down after a long shift and your knees stop aching—well, not exactly that, but you know what I mean. Then pretty quick I’m annoyed with myself.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say I’m trying to improve my mood. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually hungry when you start eating this extra food after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether I’m hungry right when it starts.\n\nSometimes after a shift I could probably eat, sure, but other times it’s more like I just pull in or grab something because it’s on the way. Like, I’ll think about dinner at home, then I’m remembering we’re out of milk or the dog needs food—well, not the dog, we don’t have one now, but you know, errands and stuff—and then I’ve already bought it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say I’m always hungry, because I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Is eating this extra food after work something that happens every time you work a late shift?\n\nPatient: Not every single late shift, no. I wish I could say it was that simple, like, “Oh, it only happens on Tuesdays,” or something.\n\nIt’s been… the last few months, I’d say usually at least once a week. Sometimes two or three nights, especially if the line’s been a mess or I get out late and I’m already worn down. Some weeks are better, but then it sneaks back in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating changes when you're feeling emotionally down or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anybody does, but I haven’t really connected it in my head, like “I’m sad, so now I eat more.”\n\nSometimes if I’ve had a rough day, sure, things kind of slide. But a rough day could be the machine acting up or somebody calling out, or I get home and there’s a bunch of little stuff waiting. My wife used to say I bring work home on my face, which… I guess I do.\n\nSo maybe it changes? I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you weren't driving past the burger place on your way home from work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, that place is right there, you know? You come off the main road and the sign’s lit up, and if traffic’s backed up by the light you’re just sitting there looking at it.\n\nIf it wasn’t there… maybe I’d just go home. Or maybe I’d stop somewhere else, I don’t know. There’s a gas station farther down that has those hot food things, though half the time they look like they’ve been sitting there since lunch. I used to stop there more when my schedule was different.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say for sure. The burger place definitely makes it easier, but I don’t know if it’s the whole reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still eat this extra food if you found another way to unwind after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’d like to think if I had something else to kind of shut my brain off, maybe I’d just go home and do that instead.\n\nBut then I think about it and, you know, I’ve tried little things before—not in any organized way, just like telling myself I’ll sit in the garage for a minute or watch something on TV. Half the time I end up messing with something in the house, or my phone rings, or I remember the trash has to go out. It’s funny, when my kids were younger there was always some noise going on, and now even when it’s quiet I don’t always know what to do with myself.\n\nSo… maybe another way to unwind would help. I just don’t know if it would stop it all by itself. I haven’t really tested that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this extra food if you were not alone when you got off work\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably not the same way. If somebody was with me, I’d be more aware of it, I guess.\n\nLike with my wife, I’ll sit down for dinner and kind of act like I’m not that hungry, you know, “oh, I’m fine,” that kind of thing. And then later, if she’s doing laundry or watching one of her shows, I might go out to the truck or find some excuse. It sounds stupid saying it. I just don’t really want her seeing how much I can put away.\n\nIt’s not like she’s mean about it. She’s not. She notices things, though. We’ve been married a long time, so she can tell when I’m off. But yeah, if I’m alone after work, it’s easier. If I wasn’t alone, I think I’d hold back more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once I start, that’s when it gets away from me.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the bag or the food, and part of me is saying, Roy, just close it up and go inside. Like, I know what I should do. But my hands just keep going back in. It’s like something flips over, and then I’m not really deciding each bite the way I should be.\n\nAnd it’s stupid because I can control myself at work fine, you know? I run machines, I check measurements, I don’t just do whatever. But with that food after work, once I’m into it… yeah, it feels like I’m not really in charge of how much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this extra food if you weren't working the late shifts\n\nPatient: I think the late shift has a lot to do with it, yeah. When I get off late, I’m already worn out and I’ll tell myself I’m just stopping for something quick, but then it turns into… two burgers, fries, a shake. And then I still go home and pick through leftovers out in the garage, like I’m having a whole second supper.\n\nIf I was working earlier, maybe it wouldn’t line up the same way. I don’t know. I used to work some different hours years back, and traffic was different then too—there was this one place by the plant that closed down, actually—but yeah, the late nights are when it really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would go back to normal if you were to switch to an earlier work shift?\n\nPatient: Maybe… but I don’t think it would just magically go back to normal.
